      Lycett P (1981), "Recovery nystagmus in Ménière's disease." Laryngoscope 91(10):1727–1737.
      → hydrops, the paroxysmal nystagmus phases (irritation→paresis→recovery), direction reversal → meniere().
[H21] Jongkees LBW, Maas JPM, Philipszoon AJ (1962). "Clinical nystagmography." Pract Otorhinolaryngol
      24:65–93; and Baloh RW, Honrubia V, "Clinical Neurophysiology of the Vestibular System" (caloric,
      caloric/vHIT dissociation); Halmagyi GM et al. (2017), "The video head impulse test," Front Neurol 8:258.
      → the bithermal caloric test, the CP/DP formula (canal paresis, directional preponderance), the
        low-/high-frequency decoupling (caloric vs vHIT) → caloric()/caloricBattery().
[H22] Musat GC, Preda MA, Tanase I, et al. (2025). "Inferior Vestibular Neuritis: Diagnostic Criteria,
      Clinical Features, and Prognosis — A Focused Review." Medicina (Kaunas) 61(2):361. doi:10.3390/medicina61020361.
      → NEURITIS of the INFERIOR nerve (nerveBranchLesion(ear,"inferior")). CONSISTENCY with the engine: (a) a
        TORSIONAL-DOWNBEAT nystagmus, directionally fixed, fixation-suppressed, torsion TOWARD THE HEALTHY ear (verticalBeat: v<0,
        t toward the healthy; no horizontal component); (b) vHIT — the POSTERIOR canal pathological (LARP/RALP), horizontal and
        anterior NORMAL; (c) caloric NORMAL (HC = superior nerve); (d) SVV — a tilt TOWARD the affected side
        (svv(): utricle + vertical canals → ~2.5° for the inferior, DESPITE a normal skew deviation = skew
        only from the utricle); (e) VEMP — cVEMP reduced (saccule, ipsi) / oVEMP normal (vemp(): cVEMP↓ inferior
        nerve, oVEMP↓ superior nerve). Added after validation 2026-07-10: parameters sacculeL/R, utricleL/R + functions
        svv()/vemp() (see STAGE 7 API). NOTE: standard HINTS = "normal" (the horizontal/utricular test is
        BLIND to an isolated IVN — consistent with the article; the localization is caught only by the posterior-canal vHIT + cVEMP).
[H23] van Dooren T, Starkov D, Lucieer F, Dobbels B, Janssen M, Guinand N, Pérez Fornos A, Kingma H,
      Van Rompaey V, van de Berg R (2022). "Suppression Head Impulse Test (SHIMP) versus Head Impulse Test
      (HIMP) When Diagnosing Bilateral Vestibulopathy." J Clin Med 11(9):2444. doi:10.3390/jcm11092444.
      → BVH THRESHOLDS (Bárány criteria): bilateral HC VOR gain (vHIT) < 0.6 [SHIMP < 0.5] · caloric sum of
        warm+cold for a GIVEN ear < 6 °/s (→ CAL_BILAT) · rotatory-chair gain < 0.1 with phase lead > 68° (NOT
        modelled; PRE-ERRATUM numbers — as cited by this 2022 paper; the 2023 erratum: gain ≤ 0.1, phase lead
        ≥ 15°, time constant ≤ 6 s — see the erratum note in the [H19] entry above). SHIMP = HEAD-fixed target → a normal VOR makes an ANTI-compensatory saccade (its ABSENCE =
        deficit — rule INVERTED vs HIMP); "covert-saccade killer" (covert ~5% vs ~35%) → cleaner gain (slightly
        lower → hence the lower 0.5 cutoff). Protocol: peak head velocity > 120 °/s, amplitude 10–30°. SHIMP
        implemented (N4/N7) (the candidate extension of headImpulse: same gain machinery, but presence of a saccade = HEALTHY VOR).
[H24] Edlow JA, Carpenter C, Akhter M, et al. (2023). "Guidelines for Reasonable and Appropriate Care in the
      Emergency Department 3 (GRACE-3): Acute dizziness and vertigo in the emergency department." Acad Emerg
      Med 30(5):442–486. doi:10.1111/acem.14728. PMID 37166022.
      → ED GUIDELINE (SAEM, GRADE, 15 recommendations) for acute dizziness < 2 wk. A timing-and-triggers approach
        (instead of the type of dizziness): t-EVS (positionally triggered = BPPV → Dix-Hallpike/Epley, roll/Gufoni)
        · AVS continuous (true AVS with spontaneous nystagmus → HINTS(+) ONLY if trained; pseudo-AVS without
        nystagmus → remove fixation, any gait ataxia = CENTRAL) · s-EVS (spontaneous episodic → vestibular migraine,
        posterior-circulation TIA). Do NOT use CT to exclude stroke (MRI DWI+MRA for central/equivocal; early MRI
        false-negative → repeat 48–72 h); the 5 Ds red flags (diplopia, dysarthria, dysphagia, dysphonia,
        dysmetria). An educational framing + authority for all of OTOREPO's differentiation (HINTS + BPPV);
        vestibular migraine (s-EVS) DELIBERATELY not modelled — flagged as a differential. (Since N1: the
        vmInterictal preset shows s-EVS between attacks — a normal examination, the diagnosis comes from the history.)
        TIME BANDS of the syndromes (added 2026-08-16 for the V22/D8 GRACE card — from the paper's
        taxonomy; "< 2 wk" is the guideline's range of applicability, NOT a definition of the AVS
        duration): t-EVS — short seconds-to-minutes attacks triggered by position (BPPV: a paroxysm
        < 1 min — the anchor of the Bárány 1-min threshold, see the comment at the 60 s engineXi
        window), no symptoms between attacks; AVS — a CONTINUOUS acute syndrome, lasting days;
        s-EVS — spontaneous episodes of minutes-to-hours, a normal examination between attacks.
        Ménière — also paroxysmal — is outside the s-EVS enumeration in [H24] (the paper lists
        vestibular migraine and posterior-circulation TIA); the V22 card notes this explicitly.

[H25] Hain TC, Fetter M, Zee DS (1987). "Head-shaking nystagmus in patients with unilateral peripheral
      vestibular lesions." Am J Otolaryngol 8(1):36–47. doi:10.1016/S0196-0709(87)80017-0.
      → HSN: head shaking at ~2 Hz charges velocity storage asymmetrically (Ewald-II rectification);
        after the shaking stops, a nystagmus TOWARD THE HEALTHY EAR, 5–15°/s in UVH, decaying with the storage τ;
        it persists in a COMPENSATED loss (a latent asymmetry — like the caloric, without irrigation); zero in
        BVH (symmetry). The basis of hsn() (stage N4).

[H26] Özgirgin ON, Kingma H, Manzari L, Lacour M (2024). "Residual dizziness after BPPV management:
      exploring pathophysiology and treatment beyond canalith repositioning maneuvers." Front Neurol
      15:1382196. doi:10.3389/fneur.2024.1382196. PMID 38854956.
      → a SUCCESSFUL maneuver ≠ a well patient: residual dizziness (RD) in 31–61% after the nystagmus has
        resolved; the mechanisms include INCOMPLETE CENTRAL COMPENSATION + UTRICULAR dysfunction (measurable with
        SVV/oVEMP); vestibular suppressants are HARMFUL (they delay compensation — in the engine: lower comp and
        the SPV comes back); counselling cuts RD to ~13%. The basis of the 'residual' preset + the RD localization
        branch (stage N5).

[H27] Bhattacharyya N, Gubbels SP, Schwartz SR, et al. (2017). "Clinical Practice Guideline: Benign
      Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo (Update)." Otolaryngol Head Neck Surg 156(3_suppl):S1–S47.
      doi:10.1177/0194599816689667. PMID 28248609. The "80%" figure is carried explicitly by the
      accompanying Plain Language Summary of the same guideline family: Bhattacharyya N,
      Hollingsworth DB, Mahoney K, O'Connor S (2017), Otolaryngol Head Neck Surg 156(3):417–425.
      doi:10.1177/0194599816689671.
      → CRP EFFICACY: a single repositioning maneuver abolishes the symptoms in ~80% of patients; NNT 1–3;
        the percentage rises with repetition (series with repeated CRPs; do NOT cite "80–93%" as the range
        of a single session — the upper values come from REPETITIONS, source verification 2026-08-16).
        The basis of the clinical row of the Debris cloud card (D9/V20).

[H28] Newman-Toker DE, Kerber KA, Hsieh YH, Pula JH, Omron R, Saber Tehrani AS, Mantokoudis G, Hanley DF,
      Zee DS, Kattah JC (2013). "HINTS outperforms ABCD2 to screen for stroke in acute continuous vertigo
      and dizziness." Acad Emerg Med 20(10):986–996. doi:10.1111/acem.12223. PMID 24127701.
      → The source for the "plus" in HINTS+ (Block 12 of the UI document): adding a hearing screen to the
        head-impulse / nystagmus / test-of-skew triad. NEW unilateral hearing loss in an acute vestibular
        syndrome raises suspicion of an AICA-territory infarct (the labyrinth is supplied from that
        territory), so it enters the result as an ALARM FEATURE. The direction works ONE WAY ONLY: the
        presence of hearing loss raises concern, its absence excludes nothing (labyrinthitis produces the
        same picture) — which is why, in the OTOREPO model, the answer "symmetric hearing" carries a
        NEUTRAL weight rather than "supports peripheral". Together with [H8] (HINTS/INFARCT), [H9] (context
        and limits) and [H24] (GRACE-3: gait ataxia = central; HINTS inaccurate without training) it forms
        the complete basis for Block 12.

[H29] Hilton MP, Pinder DK (2014). "The Epley (canalith repositioning) manoeuvre for benign paroxysmal
      positional vertigo." Cochrane Database Syst Rev (12):CD003162. doi:10.1002/14651858.CD003162.pub3.
      PMID 25485940.
      → RCTs (meta-analysis): complete resolution of vertigo OR 4.42 (95% CI 2.62–7.44; 5 trials,
        273 participants; proportions 56% vs 21% Epley vs sham/control) · conversion of Dix-Hallpike
        to negative OR 9.62 (95% CI 6.0–15.42; 8 trials, 507) · Epley ≈ Semont/Gans (no difference) ·
        Epley > Brandt-Daroff OR 12.38 (reinforces the home note V18: BD weaker than repositioning) ·
        nausea during treatment 16.7–32% (consistent with the D5 bar) · recurrence ~36%. The RCT
        context ≠ the clinical percentage [H27] — different measures.

[H30] Porwal P, Adarsh VR, Pawar V, Dorasala S, Bijlani A, Nair P, Nayar R (2021). "Clinical and VNG
      Features in Anterior Canal BPPV - An Analysis of 13 Cases." Front Neurol 12:618269.
      doi:10.3389/fneur.2021.618269. PMID 33776883. PMC7988206 (CC BY, full text read).
      -> A retrospective series of 13 AC-BPPV patients drawn from 1350 dizzy patients across 4 centres
        over 3 years; the diagnosis was confirmed by RESOLUTION of symptoms after the Yacovino maneuver.
      -> KEY FOR US - THE ROLL TEST (the authors call it McClure-Pagnini, MCP) CAN BE POSITIVE IN AC-BPPV.
        Table 2, per patient (reproduced independently from PMC and from Frontiers, identically): the MCP
        was performed in 9 of 13; positive in 6/9 - DOWNBEAT in 4 (cases 1, 3, 4, 5; PURE downbeat in 1
        and 3, downbeat plus a horizontal component in 4 and 5), purely TORSIONAL in 2 (cases 8, 10);
        negative on both sides in 3.
      -> ONE-SIDEDNESS: "MCP lateral position on one side is sensitive to only one side AC BPPV" - the
        lateral position is sensitive to one side only. This matches the engine's measurement (0.1185
        with the affected ear down vs 0.0003 with the healthy one - practically unilateral).
      -> THE AUTHORS THEMSELVES: "The role of the MCP test in AC BPPV has not been clarified" - the
        literature is thin here, so the engine's agreement with this series is agreement with a THIN base.
      -> SOURCE LIMIT (important): the paper does NOT give the MCP geometry - only the test's name, with
        no head-elevation angle. No angle can be extracted from it. The denominator is 9 (not 13) - the
        MCP was omitted in 4 patients without a stated reason; n=13 out of 1350, so no population rates.
[H31] Castellucci A, Malara P, Martellucci S et al. (2020). "Feasibility of Using the Video-Head Impulse
      Test to Detect the Involved Canal in Benign Paroxysmal Positional Vertigo Presenting With
      Positional Downbeat Nystagmus." Front Neurol 11:578588. doi:10.3389/fneur.2020.578588.
      PMID 33178119. PMC7593380 (full text).
      -> 59 cases of positional downbeat attributed to VERTICAL-canal BPPV (46 apo-PC, 7 AC, 6
        undetermined): the nystagmus was PURELY VERTICAL, with no torsion at all, in 42.4%; among
        confirmed AC-BPPV the torsion was absent in 57.1%; neither canal nor side could be determined
        in 10.1%.
      -> MEANING FOR THE MODEL: this justifies the CLINICAL MASK NYS_SHOW.anterior.t=false ("pure
        downbeat"). The engine's zero AC torsion is not a simplification against the clinic - in most
        patients with confirmed AC-BPPV no torsion is in fact seen. The atlas geometry gives t=0.739,
        so the mask is a DELIBERATE convention, not missing geometry.
[H32] Garaycochea O, Perez-Fernandez N, Manrique-Huarte R (2022). "A novel maneuver for diagnosis and
      treatment of torsional-vertical down beating positioning nystagmus: anterior canal and
      apogeotropic posterior canal BPPV." Braz J Otorhinolaryngol 88(5):708-716. PMC9483926.
      -> A prospective cohort of 157 BPPV patients; 20 (12.7%) with torsional-vertical downbeat, the side
        unambiguous in only 10 (6.35%). In AC-BPPV the upper pole of the eye beats toward the AFFECTED
        ear and the torsion is WEAKER than the vertical component; in the apogeotropic variant of the
        POSTERIOR canal the torsion beats toward the HEALTHY ear - hence a right-sided apo-PC looks
        IDENTICAL to a left-sided AC.
[H33] Bertholon P, Bronstein AM, Davies RA, Rudge P, Thilo KV (2002). "Positional down beating nystagmus
      in 50 patients: cerebellar disorders and possible anterior semicircular canalithiasis."
      J Neurol Neurosurg Psychiatry 72(3):366-372. PMID 11861698. PMC1737794 (ABSTRACT read).
      -> 50 consecutive patients with positional downbeat (downbeat was the INCLUSION CRITERION, so the
        paper cannot prove that AC-BPPV produces downbeat - it shows what such a downbeat looks like);
        in the idiopathic group (12) a torsional component in 6, weak and often clinically invisible;
        provocation frequently BILATERAL on Dix-Hallpike (9/12) or only in the straight head-hang (2/12).
[H34] Imai T, Uno A, Yamato A, Takimoto Y, Sato G, Matsuda K et al. (2014). "Comparison of the efficacy
      of the Epley maneuver and repeated Dix-Hallpike tests for eliminating positional nystagmus."
      PMID 24720888 (Auris Nasus Larynx / Class II, multicentre randomised non-inferiority trial).
      -> 180 patients with posterior-canal canalithiasis: 90 Epley vs 90 REPEATED DIX-HALLPIKE TESTS
        (up to 3 repetitions, WITHOUT any repositioning maneuver). The diagnostic test alone abolished
        the positional nystagmus THE SAME DAY in 90/90 (100%; 80 patients needed 1 repetition, 10
        patients 2), and at one week it was NON-INFERIOR to Epley (47.8% vs 50.0% cured).
      -> MEANING: this is the strongest evidence that A DIAGNOSTIC TEST CAN ACT AS A TREATMENT. It
        concerns the POSTERIOR canal and the Dix-Hallpike test ONLY - carrying it over to the horizontal
        canal and the Roll test is reasoning by analogy, not a citation. It supports the user's ruling of
        2026-08-16: "the fact that the Roll test can cure is not an error - it is to be noted".
[H35] Imai T, Okumura T, Nishiike S, Takeda N, Ohta Y, Osaki Y, Sato T, Inohara H (2018). "Recovery of
      positional nystagmus after benign paroxysmal positional vertigo fatigue." PMID 30324405
      (ABSTRACT read).
      -> THE COUNTERPOINT TO [H34]: in 20 patients with posterior-canal canalithiasis the nystagmus on an
        IMMEDIATELY repeated Dix-Hallpike falls almost to zero (mean max SPEV 48.0 -> 4.8 deg/s), but
        after 30 minutes it RETURNS to a value indistinguishable from baseline.
      -> MEANING: a negative control test IMMEDIATELY after the provocation does NOT prove repositioning -
        it may be fatigue, not cure. This sentence is in the maneuver note (poseNote) and in the session card.
[H36] Hentze M, Hougaard DD, Kingma H (2025). "Impact of head orientation and head movement in
      traditional manual diagnostics of benign paroxysmal positional vertigo: a randomized controlled
      crossover study." Front Neurol 16:1654404. doi:10.3389/fneur.2025.1654404. PMC12531064
      (full text, open access).
      -> The ONLY paper found that links a MEASURED (6DOF IMU) head-pitch angle in the supine roll test
        with detection of the nystagmus: 198 enrolled, 192 analysed for the SRT, but only 15 diagnoses of
        lateral-canal BPPV. TRUE POSITIVES had the head raised LESS (MEAN -69.0 deg, 95% CI -73.8..-64.2;
        right SRT) than FALSE NEGATIVES (-63.5 deg), and the authors recommend exceeding the -60 deg
        target TOWARD -75 deg.
      -> THE DIFFERENCE IS NOT STATISTICALLY SIGNIFICANT (p=0.128 right, p=0.661 left) and n=15 - a
        signal, not proof.
      -> MEANING FOR V25: in our convention -60 deg = 30 deg of flexion, -69 deg = 21 deg, -75 deg = 15 deg.
        The only empirical measurement of this angle therefore points in the SAME DIRECTION as the V25
        change (less flexion = better detection), though it does not reach 10 deg and is not significant.
        Do NOT cite it as confirming the figure 10.30 - cite it as directional agreement with the lack of
        significance stated openly.
[H37] Hentze M, Hougaard DD, Kingma H et al. (2025). "The Intra-Examiner Variability in and Accuracy of

[H38] Bhandari A, Bhandari R, Kingma H, Strupp M (2021). "Diagnostic and Therapeutic Maneuvers for

[H39] Bhandari R, Bhandari A, Zee DS, Nuti D, Kingma H, van de Berg R (2025). "Proposal of a standardized
      testing protocol for BPPV: Using 3D simulations for insights into movement of otoliths during
      positional tests." Eur Arch Otorhinolaryngol 282 (ABSTRACT read).
      -> DIAGNOSTIC maneuvers move the debris into new positions and therefore change the result of the
        following tests (an "order effect"); hence the recommendation to begin with the supine roll test.
        The Dix-Hallpike can displace debris ALREADY PRESENT in the horizontal canal; the roll test does
        not move debris in the vertical canals. This is independent, simulation-based confirmation of R10
        and of phenomenon No. 1 in the V25 entry.
[H40] Katsarkas A, Smith H, Galiana H (2000). "Head-shaking nystagmus (HSN): the theoretical explanation
      and the experimental proof." Acta Otolaryngol 120(2):177-181. doi:10.1080/000164800750000865.
      PMID 11603767.
      -> n=51 with a peripheral deficit (43 partial, 11 total unilateral), whole-body sinusoidal
        stimulation at 1/6 Hz over 90-220 deg/s. (1) A bias arises in every patient, but its DIRECTION
        wanders to either side until the velocity exceeds ~180 deg/s; above the threshold the slow phase
        lies toward the AFFECTED side in all patients with a bias >5 deg/s — hence the clinical lability
        of the first HSN phase's direction. (2) The monophasic and biphasic character of HSN reflects an
        interaction of TWO time constants: "velocity storage" and "gaze holding". The basis for the
        HSN_VEL_MIN threshold and the hsnPhase2() function (stage V26).

[H41] Guinand N, Pijnenburg M, Janssen M, Kingma H (2012). "Visual acuity while walking and oscillopsia

        oscillopsia severity scores in patients with BV (P>.05 for all comparisons)" — acuity loss does

        2019 PMID 31105632, Anson 2018 PMID 29599743). This paper must therefore NEVER be placed beside
        the 0.3 threshold or the 0.6/0.2 constants — only beside the order of magnitude of acuity loss
        and beside the 81%/9% contrast.

[H42] Hall CD, Herdman SJ, Whitney SL, Anson ER, Carender WJ, Hoppes CW, Cass SP, Christy JB, Cohen HS,
      Fife TD, Furman JM, Shepard NT, Clendaniel RA, Dishman JD, Goebel JA, Meldrum D, Ryan C, Wallace RL,
      Woodward NJ (2022). "Vestibular Rehabilitation for Peripheral Vestibular Hypofunction: An Updated

      -> A GUIDELINE, not a case series: a systematic review of 6 databases through June 2020, 67 papers.
        Recommendation 2 — clinicians SHOULD offer vestibular rehabilitation in unilateral and bilateral
        hypofunction (evidence quality I, strength STRONG). Recommendation 4 — do NOT use saccades or
        pursuit IN ISOLATION, without head movement (I, STRONG, harm outweighs benefit), with an explicit
        note that this does not concern COMPENSATORY saccades after a head impulse. Compensation is an
        UMBRELLA term there (adaptation of residual gain + substitution + habituation + postural control) —
        which is why the card must say "central compensation ALONE", otherwise it contradicts the guideline.
        The reason for rewriting the DVA sentence in stage V27.

[H43] Schubert MC, Migliaccio AA, Clendaniel RA, Allak A, Carey JP (2008). "Mechanism of dynamic visual
      acuity recovery with vestibular rehabilitation." Arch Phys Med Rehabil 89(3):500-507.
      doi:10.1016/j.apmr.2007.11.010. PMID 18295629.
      -> THE PAPER THAT SETTLES WHICH gain improves. n=5 patients + 4 controls, on average 5 visits over
        66 days: DVA improved in everyone (mean 51%), aVOR gain during ACTIVE head rotation rose in every
        patient, while gain during the PASSIVE head thrust did NOT improve significantly. This is the only
        ground on which the card may still claim the deficit does not vanish — because headImpulse() models
        the PASSIVE impulse. It is equally the reason the word "permanent" was dropped: Lacour 2020
        (PMID 31637477) showed a rise in PASSIVE gain too when rehabilitation began within 2 weeks.

[H44] Geisinger D, Elyoseph Z, Zaltzman R, Mintz M, Gordon CR (2024). "Functional impact of bilateral
      vestibular loss and the unexplained complaint of oscillopsia." Front Neurol 15:1365369.
      doi:10.3389/fneur.2024.1365369. PMC11070540.
      -> RESCUES THE 0.3 THRESHOLD AS A BINARY CLASSIFIER while REFUTING gain as a predictor. n=15 with a
        peripheral bilateral deficit: 5/15 (33%) reported oscillopsia; between reporters and non-reporters
        there was NO difference in VOR gain in ANY of the 6 canals (p>0.05 six times), yet DVA loss differed clearly:
        4.5±0.79 versus 2.6±0.56 lines (p<0.01). So the literature rejects "greater loss -> more severe
        oscillopsia" (see [H41]) while SUPPORTING "greater loss -> higher probability of the complaint" —
        and the latter is what the engine does. The authors' claim that oscillopsia requires a deficit of
        PERIPHERAL and sudden rather than central and progressive origin is NOT modelled here (an open
        limit of stage V27).

[H45] Karabulut M, Van Laer L, Hallemans A, Vereeck L, Van Rompaey V, Viechtbauer W, Melliti A,

[H46] Lempert T, Olesen J, Furman J, Waterston J, Seemungal B, Carey J, Bisdorff A, Versino M,
      Evers S, Kheradmand A, Newman-Toker D (2022). "Vestibular migraine: Diagnostic criteria
      (Update)." J Vestib Res 32(1):1-6. doi:10.3233/VES-201644. PMID 34719447. PMC9249276.
      Licence CC BY-NC 4.0 — NonCommercial; this is not CC BY, so a verbatim reprint of the
      criteria in a commercial product needs a separate decision (the engine PARAPHRASES them).
      -> THE DIAGNOSTIC CRITERIA FOR VESTIBULAR MIGRAINE — a joint document of the Committee for
        Classification of Vestibular Disorders of the Bárány Society and the Migraine
        Classification Subcommittee of the IHS. An update of the 2012 paper in which THE CRITERIA
        THEMSELVES WERE LEFT UNCHANGED — the literature review is new, particularly in the comments
        section ("particularly", not "only"); citing the criteria to this paper means citing 2012
        content.
        THE DEFINITE FORM, four criteria: (A) at least 5 episodes of vestibular symptoms of
        moderate or severe intensity, lasting 5 min - 72 h; (B) migraine with or without aura per
        ICHD-3, current or past; (C) at least one migraine feature in at least half of the
        episodes — headache with at least two of four features (unilateral, pulsating, moderate
        or severe, aggravated by routine physical activity) OR photophobia TOGETHER with
        phonophobia, OR visual aura; (D) not better accounted for by another vestibular diagnosis
        or by another ICHD diagnosis.
        THE PROBABLE FORM HAS THREE CRITERIA, NOT FOUR: (A) as above; (B) ONLY ONE of criteria B
        and C of the definite form is fulfilled — either the migraine history or the migraine
        features during the episode, not both; (C) no better explanation. The role of criterion D
        is carried by C here, because B and C of the definite form are condensed into one.
        INTENSITY (definitions from the source): moderate = symptoms interfere with but do not
        prohibit daily activities; severe = daily activities cannot be continued. The criteria
        provide no "mild" grade (it would not satisfy criterion A). QUALIFYING symptoms are the vestibular ones of the Bárány
        classification: spontaneous vertigo (internal and external), positional, visually induced
        and head motion-induced — while dizziness alone qualifies ONLY as head motion-induced
        dizziness WITH NAUSEA (the nausea is a condition). DURATION DISTRIBUTION: about 30% of
        patients have attacks lasting minutes, 30% hours, 30% several days, and 10% only seconds —
        in the last group the episode duration is taken as THE WHOLE PERIOD OVER WHICH the brief
        in the last group the episode duration is taken as THE WHOLE PERIOD OVER WHICH the brief
        attacks recur, not the length of a single one. In some patients full recovery takes up to
        four weeks, but THE CORE of the episode rarely exceeds 72 h — and it is the core that
        criterion A counts.
        SECTION 4.7 — THE MOST IMPORTANT ONE FOR OTOREPO: vestibular migraine MAY BE PURELY
        POSITIONAL and then it MIMICS BPPV. Three axes of separation, all clinical: its positional
        nystagmus is USUALLY PERSISTENT, of CONSTANT velocity, mostly low to moderate, rarely
        exceeding 30°/s (the contrast with the crescendo-decrescendo of free debris is OURS — the
        source describes only the persistence and the constant velocity, and never uses the word
        "crescendo"); the SYMPTOMATIC PERIOD — not the single attack — tends to be SHORTER (minutes
        to days, not weeks) and the recurrences MORE FREQUENT (several times per year versus once
        every few years).
        DIRECT OBSERVATION OF THE NYSTAGMUS IN THE ACUTE PHASE may be required to settle it.
        The converse relation is in the same paragraph: typical BPPV is MORE COMMON in patients
        with migraine, for unclear reasons — the paper gives no figure here, so this must not be
        turned into a claim about absolute frequency; the practical conclusion is: "it is migraine"
        does not excuse skipping the positional test, and a positive Dix-Hallpike does not rule out
        coexisting vestibular migraine.
        the exception, so "it is migraine" does not excuse skipping the positional test, or vice
        versa.
        SOURCE BOUNDARIES — measured in the full text while adding this entry, not assumed:
        (1) the paper DOES NOT USE the term "central positional nystagmus" and does not split the
        attack nystagmus into central and peripheral; the description quoted above is the ONLY
        descriptive handle it gives. (2) THE ICHD-3 ITEM NUMBER (A1.6.6) IS NOT IN THIS PAPER —
        it only says the entity entered the ICHD-3 APPENDIX (2013 beta and 2018) as the first step
        for new diagnoses; the number needs its own source. The probable form is not in ICHD-3 at
        all. (3) Section 4.5 DOES NOT SAY the interictal examination is normal: it says findings
        can be pathological during or shortly after an episode, and that interictal results are
        NOT SUFFICIENTLY SPECIFIC to serve as a criterion — which is not the same as "normal".
        (4) The instrumental rule works ONLY in the ruling-out direction: PROFOUND ABNORMALITIES in
        the symptom-free interval — SEVERE hearing loss or complete unilateral or bilateral
        vestibular loss — USUALLY indicate ANOTHER cause. MIND THE ADJECTIVE, because the first
        version of this entry got it wrong: in the source "profound" qualifies the ABNORMALITIES,
        while the hearing loss is "severe" (71-90 dB), not "profound" (> 90 dB). Moving the
        adjective onto the wrong noun tightened the rule by a whole audiometric grade and collided
        with section 4.6, which states that hearing loss in vestibular migraine does NOT progress to
        profound levels — a profound threshold would make the rule nearly tautological.
        (5) The paper gives NO percentage for instrumental testing and
        never names vHIT, VEMP, SVV or VNG — the diagnosis rests entirely on the clinical picture
        as reported by the patient; there is no biomarker.
        DIFFERENTIAL RULINGS STRAIGHT FROM THE TEXT: when the criteria for Menière's disease are
        met — especially with audiometrically documented unilateral hearing loss — MENIÈRE'S is
        diagnosed even in the presence of migraine symptoms (headache, photophobia and even auras
        are common during Menière attacks). BOTH diagnoses are made ONLY in a patient who has TWO
        DIFFERENT TYPES OF ATTACKS: one fulfilling the vestibular-migraine criteria, the other
        Menière's — that condition dropped out of the first version of this entry, which made the
        rule read as "Menière always wins". Vertebrobasilar TIA is considered especially in older
        patients, and the features favouring it are: vascular risk factors, sudden onset, a TOTAL
        history of attacks shorter than one year, and angiographic or Doppler evidence of vascular
        pathology in the vertebral or proximal basilar artery; sudden neck pain should raise
        vertebral artery dissection. Vestibular paroxysmia gives attacks of seconds recurring many
        times a day, responding to carbamazepine. THE COUNTER-RULE FOR A NAIVE CLASSIFIER: caloric
        stimulation OFTEN triggers a migraine attack within 24 h in patients with migraine, so
        migraine symptoms AFTER VESTIBULAR PROVOCATION DO NOT PROVE this diagnosis (criterion C
        speaks of spontaneous attacks). Epidemiology: 1-2.7% of the general population, 11% of
        dizziness-clinic patients, 13% of headache-clinic patients; 65-85% are women. More than half
        have a comorbid psychiatric disorder — that last figure comes from the section on
        psychiatric dizziness, not from the epidemiological one.
      → VERIFIED AGAINST THE ORIGINAL VERSION (E0, 2026-08-21). The sentence "the criteria of 2012
        were left unchanged" had until now been REPORTED FROM THIS PAPER. Having obtained the full
        text of the 2012 version (Lempert T et al., J Vestib Res 22(4):167–172,
        doi:10.3233/VES-2012-0453, PMID 23142830), both texts were compared: the criteria are
        IDENTICAL WORD FOR WORD, with the SINGLE exception of "ICHD" → "ICHD-3" in criterion B of
        the definite form. The preface of the 2022 version confirms this. The 2012 version GETS NO
        number of its own (user's decision, 2026-08-21): the criteria are cited from this paper as
        their current carrier, and 2012 serves as EVIDENCE, not as a citation source.
        OUTSIDE the criteria the versions DO differ: the 2012 introduction says "up to 1%" of the
        general population, the 2022 introduction "up to 2.7%" — but the epidemiology section of
        the 2022 version gives the RANGE 1–2.7%, which is what the entry above carries, so the
        agreement is complete. The aura section was renamed ("basilar-type migraine" → "migraine
        with brainstem aura, formerly termed basilar-type"), and the childhood section was widened
        to include vestibular migraine of childhood (see H55).

[H47] Bisdorff A, Von Brevern M, Lempert T, Newman-Toker DE (2009). "Classification of vestibular
      symptoms: Towards an international classification of vestibular disorders — First consensus

      LICENCE: the publisher's PDF states "IOS Press and the authors. All rights reserved" — free
      to read (bronze OA) but WITHOUT an open licence; reprinting is excluded, paraphrase mandatory.
      → THE DICTIONARY OF VESTIBULAR SYMPTOMS — the first ICVD document and the basis every other
        ICVD paper draws on. Four top-level categories: 1. Vertigo, 2. Dizziness, 3. Vestibulo-visual
        symptoms, 4. Postural symptoms — 31 entries in all, each with a definition and a comment.
        VERTIGO splits into spontaneous and triggered (positional, head-motion, visually induced,
        sound-induced, Valsalva-induced, orthostatic); DIZZINESS has a symmetric split. Category 3
        (oscillopsia, visual lag, visual tilt, movement-induced blur) and category 4 (unsteadiness,
        directional pulsion, balance-related near-fall and fall) have NO counterpart in the OTOREPO
        string layer.
        REJECTED TERMINOLOGY: the paper runs 18 blocks of "Terms not used in this nomenclature" —
        25 terms explicitly rejected. MEASURED 2026-08-21: at least EIGHT later ICVD papers
        themselves use terms from that list, some inside the text of a criterion. The terminological
        drift is therefore a property of ICVD AS A WHOLE, not a defect of our program — and must be
        described as such.
        SOURCE BOUNDARIES, measured by grep over the full text: the string "central" = 2 hits, both
        outside any criterial sense ("central processing" in the description of the system; a
        laboratory population listed in comment 3.4) — THE PAPER DEFINES NO SYMPTOM AS CENTRAL OR
        PERIPHERAL and gives no rule for telling them apart; on the contrary, its consensus principle
        states plainly that no vestibular symptom carries a fully specific topological or nosological
        meaning. The string "ICD" = 0 hits, "ICHD" = 1 (the expansion of the abbreviation only, with
        no item number). TREATMENT: "treat"/"manag"/"Epley"/"repositioning" = 0 hits each — the paper
        does not discuss treatment at all. INSTRUMENTAL TESTING: "MRI"/"caloric"/"audiome"/"vHIT"/
        "Hallpike"/"VEMP" = 0 hits each; "SVV" = 1, in a DELIMITING sentence. EPIDEMIOLOGY:
        "prevalen"/"inciden" = 0, the character "%" = 0 occurrences in the whole file.
        THE TIMING-AND-TRIGGERS TAXONOMY DOES NOT BELONG TO THIS PAPER, although Newman-Toker is a
        co-author: "vestibular syndrome"/"acute vestibular"/"episodic vestibular"/"AVS"/"EVS"/
        "timing"/"red flag"/"GRACE" = 0 hits each. The AVS/EVS axis MUST NOT be cited from this

[H48] von Brevern M, Bertholon P, Brandt T, Fife T, Imai T, Nuti D, Newman-Toker D (2015). "Benign
      paroxysmal positional vertigo: Diagnostic criteria — Consensus document of the Committee for
      the Classification of Vestibular Disorders of the Bárány Society." J Vestib Res
      25(3–4):105–117. doi:10.3233/VES-150553. PMID 26756126.
      LICENCE: the publisher's PDF states "IOS Press and the authors. All rights reserved" — as above.
      → THE BPPV CRITERIA — THE DOCUMENT THE WHOLE APPLICATION STANDS ON. Until 2026-08-21 this paper
        had NO number in the program: `baranyClassify` cited it by surname alone, `barany-check.mjs`
        pointed at an engine_doc section that did not exist, and view_doc named "otoneurology memory"
        as the source of the criteria. This entry closes that memory-based provenance.
        STRUCTURE: section 2 = ESTABLISHED syndromes, section 3 = EMERGING and atypical ones. Every
        subtype has four criteria A–D; criterion D is worded identically across eight sets ("not
        attributable to another disorder", 8 hits) and allows a comorbid condition provided it can
        be CLEARLY DISTINGUISHED.
        2.1 POSTERIOR CANAL CANALOLITHIASIS: (A) recurrent attacks of positional vertigo or
        positional dizziness provoked by lying down or turning over in the supine position;
        (B) attack duration < 1 min; (C) positional nystagmus after a latency of ONE OR A FEW SECONDS
        on the Dix-Hallpike or side-lying (diagnostic Semont) manoeuvre — a COMBINATION of torsional
        nystagmus with the upper pole beating toward the LOWER ear and vertical nystagmus beating
        UPWARD, typically lasting < 1 min; (D) as above.
        2.2 HORIZONTAL CANAL CANALOLITHIASIS: as above, but the nystagmus follows a SHORT LATENCY OR
        NO LATENCY, on the supine roll test, horizontal, GEOTROPIC (toward the lower ear) with the
        head turned to either side, < 1 min. NOTE: absence of latency is here an EXPLICITLY ALLOWED
        criterion.
        THE NOTES CARRY HALF THE CLINICAL CONTENT and must be read together with the criteria:
        note 8 — the latency may in rare cases reach 40 SECONDS; note 10 — nystagmus duration usually
        DOES NOT EXCEED 40 s before it fades spontaneously; note 11 — a crescendo-decrescendo
        envelope; note 12 — a REVERSED nystagmus of lesser intensity may follow; note 13 — on sitting
        up, reversal is common, and FATIGABILITY on repetition is a COMMON phenomenon; note 9 — the
        torsion is more prominent in the LOWER eye, the vertical component in the UPPER eye, and with
        fixation retained the nystagmus looks predominantly torsional; note 5 (horizontal canal) —
        RAPID LATERAL HEAD ROTATIONS WHILE ERECT can also act as a trigger.
        SUBTYPE EPIDEMIOLOGY: posterior canal 80–90%, horizontal 5–30%, anterior 1–2% in large series
        (though newer reports suggest more). The right labyrinth is affected slightly more often.
        Bilateral posterior canalolithiasis is not rare and is commoner after trauma; A TRAP:
        unilateral BPPV CAN MIMIC bilateral disease if the manoeuvre is done without proper head
        positioning.
        BPPV WITHOUT NYSTAGMUS: controversial; such patients ARE CODED AS 3.4 (possible BPPV).
        ROLL TEST GEOMETRY: the paper gives a head elevation of "about 30°" — the engine computes
        10.3° (HC_FLEX_DEG). This is a KNOWN, UNRESOLVED divergence; see decision D5 in
        przesiew_icvd.md.
        CANALITH JAM and PERSISTENT GEOTROPIC DCPN: the paper DESCRIBES both in its introduction as
        phenomena of unclear pathophysiology — canalith jam as a postulated locking of the cupula,
        and persistent geotropic DCPN as a picture compatible with NEITHER mobile debris NOR debris
        on the cupula, tentatively linked to a change in the density of cupula or endolymph ("but
        this remains speculative"). They are NOT criterial subtypes — the engine marks them as
        "outside the ICVD classification" and is to keep doing so.

[H49] Strupp M, Lopez-Escamez JA, Kim JS, Straumann D, Jen JC, Carey J, Bisdorff A, Brandt T (2016).
      "Vestibular paroxysmia: Diagnostic criteria." J Vestib Res 26(5–6):409–415.
      doi:10.3233/VES-160589. PMID 28262641. PMC9249278. Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → VESTIBULAR PAROXYSMIA. An entity mentioned in the program EXACTLY ONCE — in the exclusion
        block of the migraine card (criterion D) — and without a number of its own; this entry fixes
        that.
        DEFINITE FORM, five criteria (conjunction): (A) at least TEN attacks of SPONTANEOUS vertigo,
        spinning or non-spinning; (B) duration SHORTER THAN 1 MINUTE; (C) a stereotyped phenomenology
        in the given patient; (D) RESPONSE TO CARBAMAZEPINE/OXCARBAZEPINE; (E) no better explanation.
        THE PROBABLE FORM HAS ITS OWN FULL SET, not an "N of M" construction: (A) at least FIVE
        attacks (without the word "spontaneous"); (B) duration SHORTER THAN 5 MINUTES; (C) occurring
        spontaneously OR provoked by particular head movements; (D) stereotypy; (E) no better
        explanation. THE DRUG CRITERION DISAPPEARS — note 7 sends here the patient NOT YET TREATED,
        in whom criterion D of the definite form cannot be applied.
        DOSES: carbamazepine 200–800 mg/day, oxcarbazepine 300–900 mg/day. Attack frequency ranges
        from 30/day to a few per year; the course is usually longer than 3 months.
        IMPORTANT FOR DIFFERENTIATION FROM BPPV: the triggering movements TYPICALLY DO NOT FOLLOW A
        BPPV PATTERN, and if positional manoeuvres are NEGATIVE, paroxysmia remains an important
        differential.
        BOUNDARY: the horizontal-torsional nystagmus beating toward the affected ear is described
        CONDITIONALLY (note 6: "if the patient is examined during an attack, it MAY be observed") —
        it is NOT a criterion. The paper defines no "possible" form ("possible VP" = 0 hits).

[H50] Staab JP, Eckhardt-Henn A, Horii A, Jacob R, Strupp M, Brandt T, Bronstein A (2017).
      "Diagnostic criteria for persistent postural-perceptual dizziness (PPPD) — Consensus document
      of the Committee for the Classification of Vestibular Disorders of the Bárány Society."
      J Vestib Res 27(4):191–208. doi:10.3233/VES-170622. PMID 29036855. PMC9249299.
      Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      BIBLIOGRAPHIC NOTE: the Bárány Society web page gives pages 113–125 for this paper —
      INCORRECTLY. The full-text metadata and the registry agree on 191–208. Verified 2026-08-21.
      → PPPD — PERSISTENT POSTURAL-PERCEPTUAL DIZZINESS. Five criteria A–E, all required.
        (A) dizziness OR unsteadiness OR non-spinning vertigo on MOST DAYS for 3 MONTHS OR LONGER —
        operationalised in note 2 as MORE THAN 15 OF EVERY 30 DAYS; symptoms last hours, wax and
        wane, and need not be present all day; sekunds-long "flares" ALONE DO NOT SATISFY criterion A.
        (B) symptoms exacerbated by THREE factors — ALL THREE REQUIRED: upright posture, active or
        passive motion REGARDLESS OF DIRECTION, and exposure to moving or complex visual stimuli;
        avoidance of these factors MAY count as fulfilment. (C) the disorder is PRECIPITATED by a
        condition causing vertigo or imbalance — with the percentages from note 6: peripheral or
        central vestibular disorders 25–30%, vestibular migraine attacks 15–20%, panic attacks or
        anxiety 15%, concussion or whiplash 10–15%, autonomic disorders 7%, others ~3%.
        (D) significant distress OR functional impairment. (E) no better explanation.
        MOST IMPORTANT FOR THE PROGRAM: PPPD IS NOT A DIAGNOSIS OF EXCLUSION — the paper says so
        outright, and AN ABNORMAL TEST RESULT DOES NOT RULE PPPD OUT; on the contrary, when the
        criteria are met, positive findings point to an ongoing precipitant or a comorbid illness.
        That INVERTS the logic the program applies everywhere else.
        NO PROBABLE FORM EXISTS — the subcommittee DELIBERATELY DECLINED to define one (§4.2.3,
        "probable" = 2 hits, both in the context of that refusal). Any "probable PPPD" in the program
        would be an invention beyond the source. This DIFFERS from [H46], where a probable form does
        exist.
        DIFFERENTIATION FROM MdDS (see H54): in MdDS re-exposure to passive motion TEMPORARILY
        REDUCES symptoms, whereas in PPPD it far more often INTENSIFIES them — these are the defining
        features of both entities and the axis that separates them. PPPD DOES NOT CAUSE OSCILLOPSIA.

[H51] Eggers SDZ, Bisdorff A, von Brevern M, Zee DS, Kim JS, Perez-Fernandez N, Welgampola MS,
      Della Santina CC, Newman-Toker DE (2019). "Classification of vestibular signs and examination
      techniques: Nystagmus and nystagmus-like movements." J Vestib Res 29(2–3):57–87.
      doi:10.3233/VES-190658. PMID 31256095. PMC9249296. Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → THE ICVD NYSTAGMUS DICTIONARY — the largest document in the corpus (114k characters),
        67 naming entries. Until 2026-08-21 the string "Eggers" had 0 hits in the WHOLE repository,
        even though the program describes nystagmus in its own conventions across hundreds of lines,
        and SIX red flags of central positional nystagmus stand in TWO independent literals
        (svg-screens.js and obs-model.js) WITHOUT A SOURCE — and BLOCK the treatment card. This is
        the document responsible for those flags; wiring it to them is the substance of stage E2.
        SCOPE RELEVANT TO THE PROGRAM: positional nystagmus (types, directions, geotropic/apogeotropic),
        spontaneous, gaze-evoked, head-shaking, fixation suppression, and examination techniques.
        The paper ADVISES AGAINST the term "headshaking nystagmus" and RESTRICTS the use of "Bruns" —
        the program today uses terms it advises against (to be checked in E2).
        A DIFFERENCE FROM H48, IMPORTANT AND UNRESOLVED: Eggers calls the BPPV picture a PHENOTYPE,
        whereas the entity name in von Brevern asserts a MECHANISM; the two papers also give different
        numbers for positional nystagmus durations, and FATIGABILITY is a peripheral feature in
        von Brevern but a CENTRAL CLUE in Eggers — while the engine uses it as a mechanism
        discriminator. See przesiew_icvd.md, conflicts K8 and K19.

[H52] Kim HA, Bisdorff A, Bronstein AM, Lempert T, Rossi-Izquierdo M, Staab JP, Strupp M, Kim JS
      (2019). "Hemodynamic orthostatic dizziness/vertigo: Diagnostic criteria." J Vestib Res
      29(2–3):45–56. doi:10.3233/VES-190655. PMID 30883381. PMC9249281. Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → HEMODYNAMIC ORTHOSTATIC DIZZINESS/VERTIGO. A definite and a probable form, both requiring
        AT LEAST 5 EPISODES; the probable form requires at least one symptom from a list of four.
        Orthostatic hypotension is defined as a fall in systolic pressure > 20 mmHg and/or diastolic
        > 10 mmHg within 3 MINUTES of standing up or on head-up tilt.
        A TERMINOLOGICAL DISTINCTION THE PROGRAM LACKS: ORTHOSTATIC symptoms (on assuming an upright
        posture) are NOT THE SAME as POSITIONAL symptoms (on changing head position relative to
        gravity, as in BPPV). The paper considers "postural dizziness" NON-SPECIFIC and ADVISES
        AGAINST using it without further qualification. If symptoms already begin while lying down,
        the term "orthostatic" is INADEQUATE.
        CONTACT WITH BPPV: criterion D of canalolithiasis in [H48] (note 4) explicitly requires
        DISTINGUISHING positional vertigo from orthostatic symptoms present only on arising. The
        program draws no such distinction today — the string "orthostat" has 0 hits in src/.
        ADDENDUM 2026-08-22 (re-audit K7) — both sentences above need correction; the original
        wording stands as a trace. (1) THE SOURCE ANCHOR WAS WRONG: the duty to distinguish from
        orthostatic symptoms stands in [H48] as NOTE 4 TO CRITERION A of posterior-canal
        canalolithiasis (2.1) — criterion D refers only to note 14 (the not-attributable formula).
        In subtypes 2.3 and 3.2 the same content is note 5, still attached to criterion A, and the
        not-attributable formula carries the letter C there. (2) THE ZERO-HITS SENTENCE IS VOID
        SINCE STAGE D-ORTO (d0f0a3b, 2026-08-22) — as are the words "THE PROGRAM LACKS" above: the
        distinction is drawn today by the SIXTH triage question ("ortostaza", the tool of note 1
        of this paper), and this entry's coverage map says PRESENT.

[H53] Agrawal Y, Van de Berg R, Wuyts F, Walther L, Magnusson M, Oh E, Sharpe M, Strupp M (2019).
      "Presbyvestibulopathy: Diagnostic criteria — Consensus document of the Classification
      Committee of the Bárány Society." J Vestib Res 29(4):161–170. doi:10.3233/VES-190672.
      PMID 31306146. PMC9249286. Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → PRESBYVESTIBULOPATHY — age-related bilateral vestibular hypofunction. ONE set of criteria
        A–D, with NO definite/probable/possible forms.
        AGE THRESHOLD: 60 years and over. FUNCTIONAL THRESHOLD: horizontal VOR gain in the range
        0.6–0.8 — that is, BETWEEN normal and the bilateral-vestibulopathy threshold of [H19].
        THE SUPPOSED THRESHOLD CONFLICT — SETTLED 2026-08-22 (D1), AND THE REPORT WAS BADLY POSED,
        just as it was for D2. The claim was that three papers define the same deficit with three
        numbers on three different statistics. Checked at the source, criterion by criterion:
        (1) THE GAIN CRITERIA NAME NO STATISTIC AT ALL. [H19] crit. C: „gain <0.6, measured by the
            video-HIT or scleral-coil technique"; this paper crit. B.1: „VOR gain measured by video-HIT
            between 0.6 and 0.8 bilaterally". Not a word about sums. Attributing „sum of peaks" to
            [H19] and „sum of means" to this paper was OUR error — those descriptors belong to the
            CALORIC axis, not to gain.
        (2) ON THE CALORIC AXIS BOTH CRITERION LINES USE THE SAME STATISTIC, which is why they ABUT:
            [H19] crit. C — „sum of bithermal max. peak SPV on each side <6°/sec"; this paper crit. B.3 —
            „sum of bithermal maximum peak SPV on each side between 6 and 25°/sec". Same quantity,
            same edge at 6. These are not competing thresholds but deliberately built SEVERITY BANDS.
        (3) THE SAME HOLDS ON THE GAIN AXIS: BVP <0.6, presbyvestibulopathy 0.6–0.8.
            THE CAVEAT WITHOUT WHICH (2) AND (3) ARE TOO STRONG: this paper gives THREE DIFFERENT
            boundary operators for its own thresholds — the abstract „<0.8 and >0.6" and „<25°/s and
            >6°/s" (both strict), the criteria „between ... and ..." (undecided), the discussion
            „>=0.6 and <0.8" (lower bound closed). The bands abut, but the ONE discontinuity lies
            INSIDE this paper: on the abstract's reading, exactly 0.600 and exactly 6°/s belong to no
            band at all. There is no conflict BETWEEN [H19] and this paper — that is the correct wording.
        (4) [H59] AUVP 0.7 ceased to be a criterion at D2. On the caloric axis it pins „<6°/s" to the sum
            of MEAN peak SPV — but that is NOT a third statistic, and the first version of this entry
            claimed otherwise IN ERROR. The divergence runs INSIDE [H19]: its criterion line says
            „max. peak", while its OWN note 6 — the very note the criterion refers to — derives the
            figure 6 from MEANS („the sum of the mean SPV ... per ear ... <6°/sec can therefore be
            considered a safe criterion"). [H59] repeats note 6's reading; it does not contradict it.
        CONSEQUENCE FOR THE ENGINE — NO NUMBER WAS CHANGED; RANK and PROVENANCE were. GAIN_CUT {HC 0.8}
        is the ROUNDED-UP LOWER LIMIT OF NORMAL from note 5 of [H19] (0.79 at 80 ms, 0.75 at 60 ms in 60
        healthy subjects), not a criterion; BVP_CUT 0.6 is criterion C of [H19], set by the committee
        deliberately BELOW the limit of normal. Both engine numbers are also the two edges of this
        paper's band. GAIN_CUT for the VERTICAL canals (0.7) remains WITHOUT COVERAGE: note 5 of [H19]
        says the role of vertical-canal gain „has to be further evaluated" — it is an engine calibration
        choice and is labelled as such in the code.
        BOUNDARY: the use of the range "between 0.6 and 0.8" rather than strict operators means that
        a gain of exactly 0.600 falls outside the classification — see the threshold conflict in
        przesiew_icvd.md.

[H54] Cha YH, Baloh RW, Cho C, Magnusson M, Song JJ, Strupp M, Wuyts F, Staab JP (2020). "Mal de
      débarquement syndrome diagnostic criteria — Consensus document of the Classification Committee
      of the Bárány Society." J Vestib Res 30(5):285–293. doi:10.3233/VES-200714. PMID 32986636.
      PMC9249277. Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → MAL DE DÉBARQUEMENT SYNDROME. Symptoms of self-oscillation (rocking, swaying, bobbing)
        beginning AFTER the cessation of passive motion and lasting AT LEAST 48 HOURS.
        THE DEFINING FEATURE that separates it from PPPD: re-exposure to passive motion TEMPORARILY
        REDUCES the symptoms — in PPPD exposure INTENSIFIES them (see H50). Sensitivity to complex
        visual stimuli occurs in MOST MdDS patients but is NOT one of its core features, whereas in
        PPPD it is a defining feature.
        AGAINST VESTIBULAR MIGRAINE: more than 50% of MdDS patients NEVER in their lives meet the
        migraine criteria, and the rate of migraine BEFORE the onset of MdDS does not appear higher
        than the population baseline. RULE FOR CO-DIAGNOSIS: if the criteria for both disorders are
        separately met, vestibular migraine is diagnosed IN ADDITION to MdDS, not instead of it.
        THE 48-HOUR BOUNDARY: the 2020 criterion says "> 48 h", while the same first author writes
        "at least 48 h" in the 2021 paper (H57) — see the succession conflict in przesiew_icvd.md.

[H55] van de Berg R, Widdershoven J, Bisdorff A, Evers S, Wiener-Vacher S, Cushing SL, Mack KJ,
      Kim JS, Jahn K, Strupp M, Lempert T (2021). "Vestibular migraine of childhood and recurrent
      vertigo of childhood: Diagnostic criteria — Consensus document of the Committee for the
      Classification of Vestibular Disorders of the Bárány Society and the International Headache
      Society." J Vestib Res 31(1):1–9. doi:10.3233/VES-200003. PMID 33386837. PMC9249292.
      Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → THREE CHILDHOOD ENTITIES, all carrying the condition AGE < 18 YEARS.
        VESTIBULAR MIGRAINE OF CHILDHOOD (VMC): (A) at least FIVE episodes of vestibular symptoms of
        moderate or severe intensity, 5 min – 72 h; (B) migraine with or without aura, current or
        past; (C) at least HALF the episodes with at least one migraine feature (headache with ≥ 2 of
        four features OR photophobia AND phonophobia OR VISUAL aura); (D) age < 18; (E) no better
        explanation.
        PROBABLE VMC: (A) at least THREE episodes, 5 min – 72 h; (B) EXACTLY ONE of criteria B and C
        fulfilled; (C) age < 18; (D) no better explanation.
        RECURRENT VERTIGO OF CHILDHOOD (RVC), replacing the term "benign paroxysmal vertigo of
        childhood": (A) at least THREE episodes lasting FROM 1 MINUTE to 72 h; (B) NEITHER of
        criteria B and C for VMC is fulfilled; (C) age < 18; (D) no better explanation. The paper
        STATES EXPLICITLY that RVC is not a homogeneous entity but a mixture.
        THRESHOLD DIFFERENCES AGAINST THE ADULT VERSION [H46]: episode count 5 → 3 for the probable
        form and for RVC; the LOWER TIME BOUND 5 min → 1 MINUTE in RVC. The latter matters for the
        program: in a person under 18 TWO criteria sets apply and there is NO RULE OF PRECEDENCE —
        see przesiew_icvd.md.
        NO INSTRUMENTAL REQUIREMENTS: §5.5 states outright that a normal neurological examination,
        audiometry, vestibular function and EEG — present in the earlier criteria — were NOT included,
        because nystagmus, fluctuating hearing loss and vestibular hypofunction can also occur in
        vestibular migraine.

[H56] Ward BK, van de Berg R, van Rompaey V, Bisdorff A, Hullar TE, Welgampola MS, Carey JP (2021).
      "Superior semicircular canal dehiscence syndrome: Diagnostic criteria — Consensus document of
      the Committee for the Classification of Vestibular Disorders of the Bárány Society."
      J Vestib Res 31(3):131–141. doi:10.3233/VES-200004. PMID 33522990. PMC9249274.
      Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → SUPERIOR SEMICIRCULAR CANAL DEHISCENCE SYNDROME (SCDS) — the ICVD criterial document the
        program did not have: SCDS rested in the engine on [H18] Minor 2000, a primary paper, NOT on
        a consensus.
        ONE FORM, named "definite SCDS" — probable and possible forms were CONSIDERED AND REJECTED by
        the committee (§4.1). Four criteria joined by conjunction:
        (1) at least ONE third-mobile-window symptom: bone-conduction hyperacusis OR SOUND-INDUCED
        vertigo/oscillopsia time-locked to the stimulus OR PRESSURE-INDUCED vertigo/oscillopsia OR
        pulsatile tinnitus; (2) at least ONE sign or test result: nystagmus in the plane of the
        superior canal evoked by sound or pressure change OR NEGATIVE bone-conduction thresholds at
        low frequencies OR AUGMENTED VEMP responses (low cVEMP THRESHOLDS or high oVEMP AMPLITUDES);
        (3) high-resolution temporal bone CT (preferably 0.2 mm or better) with reconstruction in the
        plane of the superior canal AND orthogonal to it, demonstrating dehiscence; (4) no better
        explanation.
        A CATEGORICAL RULE (§4.9): a diagnosis of SCDS must NEVER be based on CT alone — even
        optimised scans yield false positives, and CT OVERESTIMATES the presence of dehiscence.
        ANATOMICAL DEHISCENCE ALONE IS NOT ENOUGH.
        THE PAPER GIVES NO NUMERICAL CUT-OFF FOR VEMP AT ALL — thresholds and amplitudes are compared
        with the norms of the SPECIFIC laboratory. The engine holds the constant VEMP_SCDS = 2.0;
        that number MUST NOT be attributed to this paper.
        SYMPTOMS EXPLICITLY EXCLUDED FROM THE CRITERIA (§4.2): aural fullness, "brain fog", chronic
        imbalance, worsening of migraine. Dehiscences of the POSTERIOR and HORIZONTAL canals are NOT
        covered by these criteria (§4.12).

[H57] Cha YH, Golding JF, Keshavarz B, Furman J, Kim JS, Lopez-Escamez JA, Magnusson M, Yates BJ,
      Lawson BD (2021). "Motion sickness diagnostic criteria — Consensus document of the
      Classification Committee of the Bárány Society." J Vestib Res 31(5):327–344.
      doi:10.3233/VES-200005. PMID 33646187. PMC9249300. Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → MOTION SICKNESS. The paper defines FOUR entities, not one: an acute episode of motion
        sickness (stimulus = PHYSICAL MOTION of the person) and an acute episode of VIMS
        (stimulus = VISUAL MOTION), plus the corresponding DISORDERS: MSD and VIMSD.
        ACUTE EPISODE, criteria A–D: (A) the stimulus provokes a symptom in at least ONE of five
        categories at GREATER-THAN-MINIMAL severity — nausea/gastrointestinal disturbance,
        thermoregulatory disturbance, alertness changes, dizziness and/or vertigo, headache and/or
        eyestrain; (B) symptoms BEGIN DURING the motion and INCREASE with prolonged exposure;
        (C) they EVENTUALLY RESOLVE after the motion stops; (D) no better explanation.
        THE A–D SET IS ONE AND THE SAME for motion sickness and for VIMS — they differ ONLY in the
        kind of stimulus in criterion A.
        MSD / VIMSD, criteria A–E: (A) at least FIVE episodes provoked by the same or a similar
        stimulus; (B) reliably reproducible triggering; (C) severity DOES NOT MEANINGFULLY DECREASE
        with repeated exposure (i.e. FAILURE TO HABITUATE — the core feature); (D) symptoms lead to
        activity modification OR avoidance OR aversive anticipatory emotions; (E) no better
        explanation. PROBABLE FORM: 2–4 episodes — the ONLY graded form in the whole paper; for the
        acute episode the paper provides NO grades of certainty.
        DELIMITATION FROM MdDS (note 9): symptom onset MUST occur DURING the stimulus and may not
        begin only after it ceases — MdDS begins ONLY after the motion stops and lasts at least
        48 hours.
        RULE FOR CO-DIAGNOSIS (note 10): where it coexists with vestibular migraine, vestibular
        neuritis or PPPD, BOTH DIAGNOSES ARE TO BE MADE, not one chosen.
        MILD motion sickness, easily avoided or without functional impact, SHOULD NOT be regarded as
        a disorder (§3.4).

[H58] Kim JS, Newman-Toker DE, Kerber KA, Jahn K, Bertholon P, Waterston J, Lee H, Bisdorff A,
      Strupp M (2022). "Vascular vertigo and dizziness: Diagnostic criteria." J Vestib Res
      32(3):205–222. doi:10.3233/VES-210169. PMID 35367974. PMC9249306. Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → VASCULAR VERTIGO AND DIZZINESS — the most important ICVD document for the program's RED-FLAG
        layer, whose statements currently rest on [H24] GRACE-3 and [H8] Kattah, but NOT on a
        criterial document.
        THE ACUTE PROLONGED FAMILY (≥ 24 h): the imaging-confirmed form requires (A) symptoms ≥ 24 h,
        (B) IMAGING EVIDENCE of infarction or haemorrhage in the brain OR IN THE INNER EAR matching
        the clinical picture, (C) no better explanation. THE PROBABLE FORM REQUIRES NO IMAGING AT
        ALL — (A) plus AT LEAST ONE of: focal central signs or SEVERE TRUNCAL ATAXIA; at least one
        central HINTS component; other central oculomotor abnormalities; increased vascular risk
        (ABCD2 ≥ 4 or atrial fibrillation).
        THE DEFINITION OF SEVERE TRUNCAL ATAXIA (note 3), which the program lacks: INABILITY to
        maintain an upright SITTING or STANDING position without support; in a non-ambulatory patient
        this is tested as sitting upright on a stretcher with the arms crossed. MILD-TO-MODERATE
        ATAXIA DOES NOT EXCLUDE A CENTRAL LESION.
        THE TRANSIENT FAMILY (< 24 h) and "IN EVOLUTION" (assessed within 24 h of onset): the
        probable transient form DOES NOT INCLUDE HINTS in list B — the reason, in note 3, is that
        symptoms had already resolved in about 73% (63/86) by the time of examination, so HINTS and
        MRI are of LIMITED use in a transient vestibular syndrome.
        THE AICA TRAP (note 5): ~5% of AICA strokes produce acute prolonged vertigo with canal paresis
        and NO hearing loss, mimicking a peripheral syndrome; HINTS was negative in 5/17 (29.4%) of
        AICA infarcts.
        VERTEBRAL ARTERY COMPRESSION SYNDROME (VACS) has its own four criteria with MANDATORY
        instrumental confirmation (dynamic angiography OR transcranial Doppler during the provoking
        head movement). Subclavian steal is EXPLICITLY EXCLUDED from the classification, and the term
        "vertebrobasilar insufficiency" is DISCOURAGED.
        IMPORTANT: NINDS III and the ESO DO NOT accept isolated vertigo as a symptom of
        vertebrobasilar TIA.

[H59] Strupp M, Bisdorff A, Furman J, Hornibrook J, Jahn K, Maire R, Newman-Toker D, Magnusson M
      (2022). "Acute unilateral vestibulopathy/vestibular neuritis: Diagnostic criteria."
      J Vestib Res 32(5):389–406. doi:10.3233/VES-220201. PMID 35723133. PMC9661346.
      Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → ACUTE UNILATERAL VESTIBULOPATHY (AUVP) / VESTIBULAR NEURITIS. The criterial document for an
        entity the program models extensively (presets neuritisR/L, 148 hits of "neuronit"/"neuritis"
        in the repository) — yet until now WITHOUT a criterial document, resting on [H15] Fetter 1996
        and [H22] Musat 2025.
        THE NAME: the paper introduces AUVP alongside the traditional "vestibular neuritis"; the
        string "acute unilateral vestibulopathy" has 0 hits in src/ today. Whether the two terms are
        synonyms and which one the program should carry — see decision D3 and the naming conflict in
        przesiew_icvd.md.
        DECISION D2 — RESOLVED 2026-08-21 "PER THE PAPER", AND THE RESOLUTION TURNED OUT TO DIFFER
        FROM WHAT THE REPORT ASSUMED. It was raised as an internal contradiction: note 12 gives the
        instrumental criterion as a DISJUNCTION ("gain < 0.7 for vHIT **and/or** a side difference
        of > 0.3 **and/or** a caloric side difference of > 25%"), while the vHIT section gives it as
        a CONJUNCTION with a third condition ("the gain should be **less than** 0.7 **and** the
        side-difference **greater than** 0.3. **There should also be saccades** during the test").
        Having read BOTH passages in full together with the criteria: THIS IS NOT A CONTRADICTION
        BETWEEN TWO VERSIONS OF A CRITERION, because NEITHER NUMBER IS A CRITERION.
        CRITERION C IS QUALITATIVE and contains no number at all: "unambiguous evidence of reduced
        VOR function on the side opposite the direction of the fast phase of the spontaneous
        nystagmus". The numbers live only in note 12 and in the vHIT section — and the committee
        prefaces them with the sentence that settles the matter outright: there is SO FAR NO GENERAL
        AGREEMENT ON PATHOLOGICAL CUT-OFF VALUES for vHIT or caloric testing, calculations DEPEND ON
        THE EQUIPMENT AND ANALYSING SYSTEM, and investigations therefore HAVE TO RELY ON LABORATORY
        STANDARDS OR MANUFACTURERS' DATA. Note 12 itself calls its figures a "working approximation",
        and the vHIT section adds that vHIT gains should be seen as APPROXIMATIONS dependent on
        equipment and technique.
        THE TWO READINGS ALSO ANSWER DIFFERENT QUESTIONS: note 12 — "what counts as pathological
        across BOTH tests (vHIT or caloric)"; the vHIT section — "what counts as a SIGNIFICANT VOR
        DEFICIT on vHIT itself". Hence the conjunction and the extra saccade condition in the latter.
        WHAT FOLLOWS FOR THE ENGINE — AND IT IS THE PAPER'S OWN RECOMMENDATION, NOT OUR CHOICE:
        NEITHER number enters the engine as a criterial AUVP threshold, because the paper treats
        neither as one. Were the program ever to present "0.7" as "the ICVD threshold for AUVP",
        that would be a claim STRONGER than the source. Both may be recorded as WORKING
        APPROXIMATIONS with an explicit note about laboratory dependence — and so they are here.
        A CONDITION NEITHER OF THE TWO REPORTED READINGS CARRIED: "there should also be SACCADES
        during the test". A fall in gain without saccades is not, in that section, a description of
        a significant deficit.
        VERIFICATION: the adversarial check of the extraction `17-strupp2022-auvp.md` aborted on an
        API error on 2026-08-21. The part DECISIVE for this decision — the text of criteria A–F and
        both passages carrying numbers — was read and checked directly in the full text while taking
        D2. The remainder of the extraction still awaits full checking before stage E4b.

[H60] Seemungal BM, Agrawal Y, Bisdorff A, Bronstein A, Cullen KE, Goadsby PJ, Lempert T, Kothari S,
      Lim PB, Magnusson M, Marcus HJ, Strupp M, Whitney SL (2022). "The Bárány Society position on
      'Cervical Dizziness'." J Vestib Res 32(6):487–499. doi:10.3233/VES-220202. PMID 36404562.
      PMC9837683. Licence CC BY-NC 4.0.
      → A POSITION PAPER, NOT CRITERIA — and that is its point. The committee WITHHOLDS any diagnostic
        criteria and any specific therapy outside of research. Evidence is lacking for a mechanistic
        link between the illusion of self-motion and neck pathology or neck pain.
        WHAT THE PAPER SAYS INSTEAD: when a complex head-and-neck movement triggers spinning, the
        cause is EITHER a common vestibular entity — MIGRAINE or BPPV — OR, less often, a central
        condition, including, with acute onset, vertebral artery dissection with posterior
        circulation stroke. Migraine, including vestibular migraine, is BY FAR the commonest cause of
        neck pain occurring together with vestibular symptoms.
        THE EXCLUSION RULE (the hardest operational content): cervical dizziness can be RULED OUT if
        EITHER condition holds — (1) there is NO neck pain or discomfort; (2) the dizziness EVER
        occurs spontaneously OR is PURELY POSITIONAL. THE DIRECT CONSEQUENCE FOR THE PROGRAM: all of
        BPPV's positionality goes to BPPV by this paper's own rule.
        RESPONSE TO THERAPY CANNOT BE PART OF THE DEFINITION (§5) — top-down effects can themselves
        produce vertigo and nystagmus.
        WHAT THE PAPER DOES NOT DENY: the committee accepts that further research is needed into the
        rare cases in which neck muscle spasm is associated with a vague sense of spatial
        disorientation; the position may change as new evidence appears.
        EXPLICITLY OUT OF SCOPE: cervical vascular vertigo (regarded as a separate and exceedingly
        rare entity) and whiplash.

[H61] Kaski D, Tarnutzer AA, Agrawal Y, Carey J, Cha YH, Eggers SD, Furman J, Kim HA, Kim JS,
      Lempert T, López-Escámez JA, Magnusson M, Newman-Toker DE, Seemungal BM, Staab JP, Strupp M,
      van de Berg R, von Brevern M, Ward BK, Bisdorff A (2025). "The International Classification of
      Vestibular Disorders: Achievements, challenges, and future directions." J Vestib Res
      35(3):105–112. doi:10.1177/09574271251313803. PMID 39973600. PMC12231871. Licence CC BY.
      → A REVIEW OF ICVD AS A WHOLE — the only paper in the corpus under CC BY (the rest are CC BY-NC
        or all-rights-reserved). It is NOT a criterial document; it lists the ICVD entities together
        with those that have no published criteria yet, and reports known weaknesses.
        VALUE FOR THE PROGRAM: it says what does NOT yet exist — and the program should not run ahead
        of documents still in preparation. Among other things it announces a REVISION of the
        definition of vertigo from [H47] (declared incomplete; the correction is ANNOUNCED, NOT
        INTRODUCED) and a revision of the question whether BPPV requires a SYMPTOM or nystagmus alone
        suffices.
        A MEASURED BOUNDARY: the paper speaks of "levels of diagnostic certainty" but DOES NOT NAME
        them — the strings "definite" and "probable" have 0 hits. A unified grid of certainty levels
        therefore cannot be cited from it; the corpus contains NO harmonisation document, and the word
        "probable" means FOURTEEN different things across ICVD (measured 2026-08-22; until the K7
        re-audit this read "at least seven… including OPPOSITE things" — the D3 diagnosis refuted
        the "opposition" framing, and fourteen follows from the meaning map once the migraine row
        is split into its forms). THE CORE OF THE NON-UNIFORMITY: the same bedside HIT with
        saccades suffices in [H59] as "unambiguous evidence" for the FULL form of AUVP, while in
        [H19] it NEVER leads to measured BVP (a QUANTITATIVE measurement is required for the BVP
        diagnosis) — the tool sits on opposite sides of the full/probable line. The direction of
        the anomaly: [H19] is the ONLY case among ICVD CRITERIA in which "probable" makes a
        positive finding a NECESSARY condition; [H48], [H52], [H49] and [H20] build "probable"
        through the ABSENCE of a confirmatory test (outside the ICVD criteria it can differ: the
        AAO-HNS 1995 annex reprinted in [H20] demands audiometry for "probable" too). Any
        unification in the program will be an EDITORIAL DECISION OF OTOREPO, not a citation —
        and must be described as such.

=== DEBRIS CLOUD — the multi-particle ensemble (D9/V20, 2026-08-16) ===
ORCHESTRATION ABOVE THE ENGINE, no physics (zero edits to vestibular.js): the cloud = N=9
independent simulateCanalith simulations on ONE canonical timeline (the clinician performs ONE
maneuver — re-deriving the holds per particle would give each its own protocol and bypass the
substance of partial repositioning).
 THE GRID (ensembleSim/ENS_GRID): the midpoint quantiles of the uniform distribution on
   [0.7,1.3]·r_size — m_k = 0.7+0.6·(k+0.5)/9. SOURCE BOUNDARY: the in-vivo size distribution of
   the aggregate is UNMEASURED (see the note at H2) — the uniform band is a declaration of
   ignorance, not a measurement; do NOT calibrate it. Midpoint quantiles converge to the integral
   over the distribution (aggregate peak 1.0071/1.0085/1.0091 for N=7/9/13); a grid WITH ENDPOINTS
   overweights the extremes and drifts with N (1.061→1.035) — the old "1.085" anchor from
   assessment II is an artifact of such a coarse N=5 grid (reproduced 1.0865); the qualitative
   thesis (dispersion shifts forward and raises the peak) stands, there is NO target number. The
   grid's center (k=4) = EXACTLY 1.0 → the middle particle is BIT-EQUAL to the canonical maneuverSim.
 AGGREGATION = the arithmetic MEAN of ξ over the particles: the engine ALREADY scales gain∝r³
   inside simulateCanalith — an explicit extra r³ weight would count the mass TWICE (measured
   1.353, outside the CUP_WEAK/SPV calibration) and is FORBIDDEN. fracMass (r³) is a separate
   quantity: the fraction of MASS removed, not a signal weight.
 PARTIAL REPOSITIONING — EMERGENT from the canonical holds (zero new constants): the derivedHold
   guard TAUP_GUARD=1.1 guarantees clearing for r ≥ 1/√1.1 ≈ 0.953 — and exactly the particles with
   r<0.95 fall out of the windows. The table (medium, P, pinned in engine.ensemble): epley 8/9 (96%
   of the mass) · semont/bascule/yacovino 6/9 (84%) · lempert/gufoniGeo/zuma/kim 9/9 · gufoniApo
   0/9 (a CONVERSION — the card renders it as a conversion, not as a failure). The card teaches
   this directly: the FINEST particles remain (the slowest ones: τ ∝ r⁻², Stokes' law) — "repeat
   the positional test".
 SEPARATE AXES: rep does NOT enter the cloud (rep₂/rep₀ = fatigueFactor(2) identically to 6 digits
   for the single particle and for the aggregate — gc enters linearly, the φ trajectory is
   independent of gc) → the ensemble does NOT replace fatigueFactor (the cloud = SIZE dispersion
   within one provocation; fatigueFactor = clump disintegration BETWEEN provocations, Imai) — a
   separate calibration decision, outside V20. The canonical single simulation REMAINS the source:
   of derivedHold/timers, of manExitStep/migration, of the V10/V19 session, of symRow/RD (D5), of
   the sensitivity/EXPEL_SANE oracles. The cloud is a VIEW only (the card's numbers + the row of
   dots; drawing the cloud on the otolith path is FORBIDDEN — the manFractions ramp is schematic,
   positional realism would be fabrication).
 CANAL SWITCH = A NOTE (not orchestration): the utricle lies over the non-ampullary HC opening —
   part of HC-BPPV arises as a canal switch after treatment of the posterior canal (see R3); full
   orchestration would require a p(switch) with no source and seeding φ₀ in another canal (session
   territory) — a separate candidate. A design probe: the dynamics AFTER entry would be honest
   (HC φ₀≈250–265 at the opening, settled:false → sitting up slides the debris toward restPhi, the
   control Roll = geotropic DCPN +0.82..0.86/−0.91..−0.92, the phenotype insensitive to the depth
   of entry).
 UI: an opt-in "Debris cloud" in the #sessionbar shell (outside #app — golden untouched by
   construction), the card ONLY on the guide's final step, OUTSIDE a session (the !state.session
   gate: a session tells the story of ONE debris clump with a history). The clinical row
   DELIVERED 2026-08-16 after source verification (the user's decision): "~80% after a single
   session + rises with repetition" [H27]; the RCT context (56% vs 21%, OR 4.42) from [H29];
   the original "80–93%" REJECTED — the upper values come from REPETITIONS, not from a single
   session. Oracles: engine.ensemble (the grid + 9 maneuvers ×
   2 sides: fractions/peak/tExit per particle; a diffKeys line in --check) + the hard CLOUD ORACLE
   (throw: the center ≡ the canonical run bit-for-bit — the card cannot diverge from the eyes)
   + 3 dom keys (ensemble-on ×2, ensemble-off ≡ the baseline finale BIT-FOR-BIT = the eternal pin
   "OFF means zero bytes"; dom 174→177). Successor candidate: a cloud session (a chain per
   particle, M× cost; a control test after a partial repositioning with a response ∝ the retained
   mass) — deliberately outside V20.

SIMPLIFICATIONS / CAVEATS:
  - HORIZONTAL UVH nystagmus: torsion as a fixed fraction of the horizontal (TORS_FRAC=0.5) — illustrative; the VERTICAL canals
    (stage 7/E3) are composed realistically PER coplanar PAIR (verticalBeat), but with a simplified TORS_V=0.8;
    the HINTS RENDERER draws horizontal + VERTICAL + torsion (added in U4: neuroNysParams Av/vDir; the inferior-nerve downbeat visible);
    the animation is GATED by the visibility threshold (strength < VIS_THRESH/SPV_MAX → 0), so as not to show sub-threshold
    gaze-evoked drift of the healthy (τ=25 → 0.8°/s) — consistent with the label (spv<VIS_THRESH = "no nystagmus");
  - [EXTENDED in N7] vHIT: a BINAURAL gain (W_IN=1/3), velocity-dependent, with a pursuit term —
    still one number per impulse (without the full eyeball velocity dynamics and saccade metrics beyond amplitude);
  - a linear integrator (drift θ/τ) without horizontal/vertical asymmetry and without rebound nystagmus;
  - [RESOLVED in N7 of review II] Alexander's law: a multiplicative term ALEX_GAIN=0.02 (toward:away 2.5:1)
    + degrees I–III (alexanderGrade); it remains a LINEAR simplification without an adaptive term (rebound);
  - skew as a scalar vertical divergence; no full OTR geometry (head tilt, cyclotorsion) —
    we model the graviceptive axis as a SINGLE number (skewTone) shared by skew and SVV, without separating
    the level of the lesion (medulla=ipsiversive vs midbrain/INC=contraversive); the stroke preset assumes the
    MEDULLARY variant (Wallenberg) as the commonest vascular cause of AVS;
  - [RESOLVED in N3 of review II] fixationGain 0.65 by default → a fixation index of 0.35 (the middle of the
    clinical band 0.3–0.6); the old claim that a correction "would shift every readout" was REFUTED by probe
    (the suppressed≤0.5 and failsSuppression>0.5 thresholds have a buffer on both sides — zero flag changes);
  - pacemakerBias (Bechterew) requires an EXPLICIT lesionEar once both labyrinths have returned to symmetry: after
    the tones have equalized the affected side CANNOT be recovered from the parameters alone (the information is gone), so a
    charge without a stated side stays idle — a limitation of the model, not an error of the derivation;
  - compensation models a loss (HYPOfunction) only: gap=max(0,·). An IRRITATIVE asymmetry (the tone of the affected
    side > the healthy one) is not compensated — consistent with the clinic (a Ménière attack lasts minutes–hours,
    orders of magnitude too short for static compensation), but that is an assumption, not a result;
  - compensation: a single scalar c (without a time axis hours↔weeks); clamp/pacemaker as pure functions of c;
    the pacemaker charge (Bechterew) as a sticky parameter (a carrier of history), because the scalar c carries no memory;
  - neck-ocular fusion and velocity storage: phenomenological (weight ∝ reliability / c), without a full Kalman
    filter; HSN ADDED in N4 (a MONOphasic model — no biphasic reversal; a vertical "perverted HSN" after
    horizontal shaking DELIBERATELY not modelled: a simple flag from verticalBeat would fire falsely for a
    peripheral superior neuritis, and a correct model requires cross-coupled velocity storage);
  - HINTS as an EDUCATIONAL model — it does NOT replace an examination; valid in AVS with a persistent nystagmus,
    interpret with HINTS "plus" (hearing) and context ([H9] Tarnutzer 2011). NOTE: a compensated peripheral loss
    can have a bedside HIT "normal" (covert saccades) — a pitfall when the AVS nystagmus is no longer present.

================================================================================
OBSERVATIONS OUTSIDE THE MODEL — what the BPPV engine does NOT compute (Block 8)
================================================================================
The "Nystagmus" step lets the clinician record what they SAW in the patient. Some of the features
the form asks about have NO counterpart in the engine — and that is written down explicitly, so
that nobody mistakes the model's silence for its prediction. The "Compare with the model's
prediction" card returns the verdict `nieporownywalne` (not comparable) for them, never `rozne`
(different).

1. EFFECT OF VISUAL FIXATION. The BPPV engine has no fixation parameter. Verified: the key set of
   `Vestibular` (isExcitatory, quickPhase, nysMag, nystagmus, gHead, qSupineYaw, qPitch, position,
   simulateCanalith, simulateCupulolith, dynNystagmus, nystagmusPhase, fatigueFactor, qmul, qconj,
   qaxis, rotate, GEXC, CANAL_NORMALS, CUP_WEAK) contains not a single one. Fixation suppression
   exists EXCLUSIVELY in NeuroVOR (see [H3]) and is read only by the HINTS screen. Clinically the
   feature matters (peripheral nystagmus is suppressed by fixation, central is not), so the field
   stays in the form — with weight 1 and an explicit annotation next to the question.

2. DIRECTION REVERSAL ON SITTING UP. The model has no sitting-up phase in the diagnostic test:
